Advances in tuberculosis diagnostics and biomarkers, 2020-26: scientific evidence, recommendations, gaps, and developmental priorities.

Walzl G, McHugh TD, Chakaya J et al.. Advances in tuberculosis diagnostics and biomarkers, 2020-26: scientific evidence, recommendations, gaps, and developmental priorities.. The Lancet. Microbe. 2027

This paper reviews advances in tuberculosis diagnostics and biomarker research over roughly a five-year period, synthesizing scientific evidence to inform updated WHO policy guidance that consolidates recommendations for detecting tuberculosis disease and drug resistance into a unified framework. Digital chest radiography with computer-aided detection has become a core WHO-recommended screening and triage tool, while decentralised molecular diagnosis has expanded through a diversified portfolio of rapid nucleic acid amplification tests, including automated and manual platforms, as well as near point-of-care devices under evaluation. Novel specimen types such as tongue swabs and non-sputum specimens including stool and nasopharyngeal samples have improved bacteriological confirmation in children and others who cannot produce sputum, and targeted next-generation sequencing is now endorsed for comprehensive drug-susceptibility testing at reference laboratory level. Urine lipoarabinomannan assays improve diagnostic yield in people with HIV and advanced disease, though their broader applicability remains limited, and host blood transcriptomic and other biomarker signatures show promise for prognosis and triage but none yet meets WHO target product profile criteria for a point-of-care test that reliably distinguishes infection from disease, including subclinical tuberculosis. The paper concludes that substantial performance, implementation, and equity gaps persist across the diagnostics landscape.

Development of a microfluidic isothermal amplification nucleic acid detection (mFIND) platform for simultaneous detection of respiratory pathogens.

He P, Feng Y, Zhou X et al.. Development of a microfluidic isothermal amplification nucleic acid detection (mFIND) platform for simultaneous detection of respiratory pathogens.. Biosensors & bioelectronics. 2026

The paper describes the development of a centrifugal microfluidic platform designed to simultaneously detect multiple respiratory pathogens, including influenza A and B, SARS-CoV-2, and RSV, from a single sample. The system integrates isothermal amplification with lyophilized reagents in independent reaction chambers on a chip, paired with a portable analyzer that automates temperature control and real-time fluorescence detection, completing the process in under fifteen minutes using very small reaction volumes. The platform demonstrated extremely low limits of detection for all four target pathogens and showed no cross-reactivity against a broad panel of non-target organisms. Clinical validation against a large set of confirmed positive samples showed complete agreement with quantitative PCR for both positive and negative results. The authors conclude that the platform represents a practical point-of-care nucleic acid testing solution suitable for decentralized diagnostic settings, combining speed, simplified workflow, and multiplexed pathogen detection.

Development and evaluation of a rapid duplex qPCR assay for Treponema pallidum using the GeneSoC microfluidic platform.

Kawahata T, Banno F, Hama M. Development and evaluation of a rapid duplex qPCR assay for Treponema pallidum using the GeneSoC microfluidic platform.. Diagnostic microbiology and infectious disease. 2026

This paper describes the development and evaluation of a rapid duplex quantitative PCR assay for detecting Treponema pallidum, the causative agent of syphilis, using a microfluidic platform. The assay targeted two genomic regions simultaneously and was tested against DNA standards, a reference strain, and clinical specimens including lesion swabs and urine samples, with and without prior DNA extraction. The assay demonstrated good linearity and amplification efficiency across both targets, showed no cross-reactivity with other pathogens, and achieved detection within a substantially shorter timeframe than a conventional comparator system, owing to the faster temperature transitions enabled by microfluidic thermal cycling. Detection was possible directly from clinical specimens without DNA extraction, though extraction was noted to improve sensitivity when target DNA concentrations are low. The authors conclude that this platform represents a promising approach for point-of-care syphilis diagnosis, including during the seronegative window period when serological tests may be unreliable.

Trends of nucleic acid - based point-of-care diagnostics for infectious diseases.

Hatem H, Mysara M, Ramadan R. Trends of nucleic acid - based point-of-care diagnostics for infectious diseases.. Journal of biological engineering. 2026

This review examines the landscape of nucleic acid-based point-of-care diagnostics for infectious diseases, motivated by the global need for decentralized, rapid, and accessible testing outside conventional laboratory settings. The authors systematically organized their analysis around three core operational stages: nucleic acid extraction, amplification, and detection. For extraction, they evaluated approaches including magnetic bead-based, paper-based, and integrated microfluidic methods, emphasizing their suitability for simplified and equipment-free deployment. For amplification, they reviewed both thermal cycling methods such as polymerase chain reaction and isothermal alternatives including loop-mediated isothermal amplification, recombinase polymerase amplification, and nucleic acid sequence-based amplification, all adapted for portable and low-power conditions. On the detection side, the review spans colorimetric and fluorescent lateral flow assays through to advanced biosensor platforms exploiting CRISPR/Cas systems and toehold switch mechanisms, which offer highly sensitive and programmable nucleic acid recognition.

A nested PCR-based point-of-care multiplex test for detection of bacterial pathogens in cerebrospinal fluids.

Wu J, Zhao Z, Pang P et al.. A nested PCR-based point-of-care multiplex test for detection of bacterial pathogens in cerebrospinal fluids.. Frontiers in cellular and infection microbiology. 2026

This study developed and evaluated a multiplex one-tube nested real-time PCR assay integrated with a point-of-care testing instrument for detecting three bacterial pathogens responsible for central nervous system infections in cerebrospinal fluid. The researchers assessed analytical sensitivity using recombinant plasmids and simulated cerebrospinal fluid samples, evaluated specificity against common pathogens, and validated clinical performance using over one hundred clinical samples compared against conventional real-time PCR with manual nucleic acid extraction. The integrated point-of-care system achieved low detection limits for all three target pathogens with no cross-reactivity, and reduced total testing time to approximately two hours compared to four hours with manual methods. In clinical testing, the new assay identified additional positive cases that conventional real-time PCR had missed, demonstrating superior sensitivity. The authors conclude that this automated, culture-free workflow offers a rapid and highly sensitive diagnostic approach for bacterial meningitis pathogens directly from cerebrospinal fluid.

A Novel Voltammetric Point-of-Care Device for Rapid and Accurate Nucleic Acid and Viral Pathogen Detection.

Munita R, Lyttleton R, Danefur E et al.. A Novel Voltammetric Point-of-Care Device for Rapid and Accurate Nucleic Acid and Viral Pathogen Detection.. ACS omega. 2026

This paper investigated a novel electrochemical approach to infectious disease diagnostics designed to overcome the sensitivity limitations of lateral flow assays and the optical detection challenges of isothermal amplification methods. The researchers developed a nucleic acid amplification test that couples isothermal loop-mediated amplification with real-time voltammetric detection, using a pH-sensing redox compound to monitor the proton release that accompanies amplification. The method was evaluated against established RT-qPCR standards across several respiratory pathogens, including SARS-CoV-2, influenza A and B viruses, RSV A, and equine herpesvirus, using both purified RNA from clinical samples and an extraction-free workflow with nasal swabs. The system demonstrated high concordance with RT-qPCR results and achieved a low limit of detection for influenza A virus. The authors conclude that real-time voltammetric amplification is feasible across multiple sample types and pathogens, while acknowledging that further validation in additional matrices and prospective cohorts is needed before broad point-of-care deployment.

Biosensing technologies for foodborne pathogen detection and healthcare: principles, emerging materials, and intelligent platforms

Purshottam J. Assudani, Balakrishnan . P., Anny Leema A et al.. Biosensing technologies for foodborne pathogen detection and healthcare: principles, emerging materials, and intelligent platforms. Microchimica Acta. 2026

This review examines the current state and future directions of biosensing technologies designed to detect priority foodborne pathogens, including Escherichia coli, Salmonella, and Listeria monocytogenes, with relevance to both food safety and clinical healthcare settings. The authors survey electrochemical, optical, and microfluidic sensing platforms, evaluating how advances in functional nanomaterials, aptamers, nanozymes, and surface engineering strategies have improved sensor sensitivity, specificity, and performance in complex food matrices. A central focus is the integration of artificial intelligence and machine learning with these biosensors, enabling real-time analytics, intelligent pattern recognition, and high-throughput multiplexed detection that transforms conventional systems into smart diagnostic devices. Looking forward, the authors advocate for self-powered biosensors, modular pathogen detection panels, blockchain-based data integrity, and standardized validation pipelines as pathways toward deployable, regulation-ready platforms for next-generation food safety monitoring.

Renormalization-guided inverse blocking for lattice field generation: construction and validation

Anna Hasenfratz, Ethan T. Neil, Letizia Parato et al.. Renormalization-guided inverse blocking for lattice field generation: construction and validation. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper introduces a lattice field configuration generation algorithm that inverts a renormalization-group blocking transformation to efficiently sample field configurations across multiple length scales. The blocking transformation is optimized using a "perfect blocking" condition so that the coarse-grained lattice follows a simple action, and it is decomposed into an invertible smoothing step followed by decimation, with a conditional normalizing flow reconstructing the short-distance degrees of freedom lost during decimation. A brief rethermalization step corrects residual mismatches, and the procedure is applied recursively in a cascade, reusing the same learned transformation to upscale from small to very large volumes. Tested in two-dimensional scalar field theory at criticality, the method successfully generates configurations through cascade upscaling spanning several orders of magnitude in lattice size using only local computational resources. Rethermalization tests confirm that short-distance mismatches relax quickly, while mismatches in the relevant thermal direction relax much more slowly, consistent with expected renormalization-group behavior, and the framework is designed to generalize to higher dimensions, gauge fields, and fermionic systems.

QUEST: A Query and Extraction System for Topics in Asylum Law Application Decisions

Maria Vlachou, Anna Murphy Høgenhaug, Mohammad N. S. Jahromi et al.. QUEST: A Query and Extraction System for Topics in Asylum Law Application Decisions. arXiv. 2026 preprint

The paper addresses the challenge of automatically identifying credibility-related factors in asylum application appeal decisions, which are lengthy, complex legal documents combining interviews, original rulings, and supporting materials. The authors developed QUEST, an information retrieval system that combines synthetic query generation, topic extraction, and relevance assessment to locate information pertaining to credibility indicators within two datasets of Danish asylum appeal decisions. Beyond standard retrieval evaluation metrics, they proposed a novel domain-specific assessment framework tailored to credibility factors rather than conventional topical relevance. Their findings reveal that evaluating system performance through credibility-based relevance assessments is considerably more difficult than standard retrieval evaluation, highlighting the inherent complexity of extracting legally meaningful credibility information from asylum documents.

Offline-Verifiable Accountability for Cross-Organization Agent Messaging: A Preserved Evidence-Bundle Approach

Adil Alshammari, Hayretdin Bahsi. Offline-Verifiable Accountability for Cross-Organization Agent Messaging: A Preserved Evidence-Bundle Approach. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper addresses the challenge of verifying accountability for cross-organization agent-to-agent messaging workflows when live systems are unavailable, controlled by a single party, or not mutually trusted. The authors propose a preserved evidence-bundle model in which each workflow event carries policy-required evidence including sender authentication, log commitments, witness-backed checkpoints, append-only continuity proofs, delegation authorization, and optional receiver-signed receipts. A policy-controlled offline verifier then assesses whether a given bundle satisfies the selected policy's evidence requirements without inferring delivery or receipt from transport behavior alone. In a prototype evaluation across hundreds of complete workflows and thousands of preserved bundles, the system successfully rejected all corrupted or policy-insufficient bundles with no false acceptances, while checkpoint-context anchoring was identified as the highest-latency component. The work aims to provide a principled, verifier-centered basis for audit and dispute resolution that does not depend on platform-specific logs or online service availability.

Analysis of Polynomial Threshold Functions on Random Regular Graphs: Computational Complexity of Detecting Noisy Random Lift

Xifan Yu. Analysis of Polynomial Threshold Functions on Random Regular Graphs: Computational Complexity of Detecting Noisy Random Lift. arXiv. 2026 preprint

The paper studies the computational complexity of a hypothesis testing problem: distinguishing a noisy random lift of a base regular graph from a uniformly random regular graph. To analyze this problem, the authors develop the first treatment of low-degree polynomial threshold functions in this setting, characterizing the power and limitations of this class of computationally efficient algorithms. A key technical contribution is a new result on the distribution of short cycle counts in noisy random lifts up to logarithmic lengths, which generalizes several prior results on cycle statistics in random regular graphs and random lifts. The findings contribute to understanding which graph hypothesis testing problems are computationally tractable versus intractable for low-degree methods.

Hierarchical organization governs nonlinear mechanical reversibility in $ι$-carrageenan gels

Thomas Gibaud, Loïc Hilliou. Hierarchical organization governs nonlinear mechanical reversibility in $ι$-carrageenan gels. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper investigated how the molecular and mesoscale organization of ι-carrageenan gels controls their mechanical behavior under both small and large deformations. The researchers studied temperature-dependent rheology of these thermoreversible polysaccharide gels formed in potassium chloride solutions, using linear and nonlinear oscillatory rheology alongside normal-force measurements. They found that deeper temperature quenches produce gels with higher elastic moduli and pronounced strain stiffening before yielding, accompanied by internal stresses evidenced by negative normal forces. Strikingly, large deformations partially and reversibly altered the network by suppressing strain stiffening while leaving the small-strain elastic modulus intact. The authors interpret these findings through a hierarchical network model in which a robust intermolecular network governs linear elasticity, while a more fragile mesoscale organization enables cooperative structural alignment and stress amplification that can be reversibly disrupted under deformation.

Understanding Venture Capital Syndication in Information Technology Sectors: A Network Formation Perspective

Liheng Tan, Zhengkai Tu, Prasanna Karhade. Understanding Venture Capital Syndication in Information Technology Sectors: A Network Formation Perspective. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper investigates how venture capital co-investment networks form and evolve within the U.S. information technology sector, focusing on the mechanisms that drive syndication partnerships among investors. Using a comprehensive dyadic dataset spanning several decades across hardware, software, and hybrid subsectors, the researchers tested how factors such as prior collaboration, shared investment partners, geographic proximity, and organizational similarity influence the likelihood of two investors co-investing in a given year. They employed dyadic logit models with clustered standard errors across the full sample as well as an exponential random graph model applied to a snapshot of the software network. The findings consistently show that prior collaboration is the strongest predictor of new co-investment ties, while triadic closure and geographic homophily also robustly structure network formation, with other mechanisms like domain overlap and prominence showing more context-dependent effects. The study concludes that relational persistence, network closure, and similarity jointly govern how syndication networks are organized in information technology venture finance.

Point prevalence assessments of five respiratory viruses in adults ≥ 50 years of age in Germany by multi-pathogen rapid antigen testing on June 1, 2024, and December 15, 2024.

Anggitakirana M, Nacov JA, Salmanton-García J et al.. Point prevalence assessments of five respiratory viruses in adults ≥ 50 years of age in Germany by multi-pathogen rapid antigen testing on June 1, 2024, and December 15, 2024.. Infection. 2026

This study investigated the circulation of five respiratory viruses among community-dwelling adults aged fifty years and older in Germany using two cross-sectional point prevalence assessments conducted in summer and winter of 2024. Participants self-administered multi-pathogen rapid antigen tests capable of detecting adenovirus, SARS-CoV-2, influenza A and B, and respiratory syncytial virus, while also reporting symptoms, healthcare use, and work absenteeism. The overall pathogen detection rate was low, with the majority of tests returning negative results, but adenovirus was the most frequently detected pathogen in both assessment periods, a finding that contrasts with its typical underrepresentation in routine surveillance. SARS-CoV-2 was associated with the greatest clinical and economic burden among positive cases, driving the highest rates of healthcare consultations and work absenteeism. The authors conclude that community-based screening using multi-pathogen testing can reveal asymptomatic reservoirs and underreported viral circulation, including a potential co-infecting role for adenovirus, while providing insight into the real-world impact of respiratory viruses on older adults.

'No, it is a virus, your body will fight it.' The value of diagnostic testing for managing respiratory viruses in primary care: an observational study from a post-COVID pilot programme.

Wesley-Smith L, Dowell A, Turner N et al.. 'No, it is a virus, your body will fight it.' The value of diagnostic testing for managing respiratory viruses in primary care: an observational study from a post-COVID pilot programme.. Journal of primary health care. 2026

This study examined how laboratory-based polymerase chain reaction testing for respiratory viruses, combined with rapid antigen testing for COVID-19, influenced patient management in a primary care setting during the pandemic. Using electronic medical records from a single urban general practice in New Zealand, researchers analysed data on test results, antibiotic prescribing, patient presentations, and follow-up communications over a ninety-day window before and after testing. They found that a majority of presentations yielded a positive polymerase chain reaction result, yet antibiotic prescribing rates remained high and, notably, patients who tested positive for a respiratory virus were actually more likely to receive antibiotics than those who tested negative. While digital communication methods such as texts and emails were used to convey results to some patients, gaps in result communication and inconsistent prescribing patterns persisted. The authors conclude that broader environmental, clinical, and psychological factors limit the impact of diagnostic testing on prescribing behaviour, and suggest that faster point-of-care technologies may partially, but not fully, address these challenges.

Respiratory multi-viral rapid-antigen point-of-care tests in New Zealand primary care: a mixed-methods pilot.

Bibby S, Marsh S, Dowell A et al.. Respiratory multi-viral rapid-antigen point-of-care tests in New Zealand primary care: a mixed-methods pilot.. BJGP open. 2026

This pilot study examined whether multi-viral rapid-antigen point-of-care tests could be feasibly integrated into routine primary care in New Zealand and assessed the attitudes of both patients and clinicians toward their use. Using a prospective mixed-methods design across seven primary care clinics during winter, the researchers administered these tests to patients presenting with acute respiratory symptoms, then collected data through medical record audits, surveys, and qualitative interviews and focus groups. The tests were well-received by all clinicians who participated, with the majority reporting that results influenced their antibiotic prescribing decisions and a subset using them to guide secondary care referrals. Nearly all patients, whether testing positive or negative, expressed a desire to have access to these tests in the future, and those who tested positive reported greater intention to adopt non-pharmaceutical measures to limit transmission. The authors conclude that multi-viral rapid-antigen point-of-care tests are acceptable and practical in primary care settings and could improve management of respiratory illnesses while encouraging behaviour change to reduce spread.

Evaluation of the QuickFinder COVID-19/Flu/RSV antigen test for rapid simultaneous detection of SARS-CoV-2, influenza A/B viruses, and respiratory syncytial virus.

Kim JA, Kang M, Yu HJ et al.. Evaluation of the QuickFinder COVID-19/Flu/RSV antigen test for rapid simultaneous detection of SARS-CoV-2, influenza A/B viruses, and respiratory syncytial virus.. Diagnostic microbiology and infectious disease. 2026

This study evaluated the diagnostic performance of the QuickFinder COVID-19/Flu/RSV Antigen Test, a lateral flow immunoassay designed to simultaneously detect SARS-CoV-2, influenza A and B viruses, and respiratory syncytial virus from nasopharyngeal swab specimens. Archived clinical specimens were tested in parallel using the QuickFinder and real-time reverse transcription polymerase chain reaction as the reference standard, with cross-reactivity also assessed against a panel of respiratory pathogens. The test demonstrated high sensitivity for SARS-CoV-2 and influenza A, with somewhat lower sensitivity for influenza B and RSV, and achieved perfect specificity for all four targets with no cross-reactivity observed. Sensitivity was consistently strong in specimens with high viral loads but diminished in low viral load samples. The authors conclude that while the QuickFinder is a reliable rapid screening tool for differentiating major respiratory viruses, it should complement rather than replace molecular testing due to its reduced sensitivity when viral loads are low.

No detection of respiratory syncytial virus at one clinic in Japan with rapid immunochromatographic antigen tests for three viruses using the same nasopharyngeal swabs from adult patients

Kajiwara N, Tsunetoshi Y, Shiode S. No detection of respiratory syncytial virus at one clinic in Japan with rapid immunochromatographic antigen tests for three viruses using the same nasopharyngeal swabs from adult patients. 2026

This study investigated the disease burden of respiratory syncytial virus (RSV) infection among adult patients at a Japanese clinic by testing nasopharyngeal swabs using rapid immunochromatographic antigen tests capable of detecting SARS-CoV-2, influenza viruses A and B, and RSV simultaneously. Samples were collected from adult patients presenting with acute respiratory infection symptoms over approximately one year. While the tests successfully identified patients positive for SARS-CoV-2 and influenza A and B, no RSV-positive cases were detected among the cohort. Comparing this outcome with previous studies that used polymerase chain reaction testing, which identified RSV in a notable proportion of patients, the RSV-positive rate was significantly lower. The authors conclude that rapid immunochromatographic antigen testing has limited usefulness for diagnosing RSV infections in Japanese adults, likely due to insufficient sensitivity compared with PCR-based methods.

Can exercise affect immune function to increase susceptibility to infection?

Richard J. Simpson, Richard J. Simpson, Maree Gleeson et al.. Can exercise affect immune function to increase susceptibility to infection?. PubMed. 2026

This debate article examined the contested question of whether exercise itself can suppress immune function and increase susceptibility to infection. The paper structured opposing arguments around several key issues, including whether high-performance athletes are genuinely more infection-prone than the general population, whether exercise independently alters immunity beyond the effects of confounding factors such as sleep disruption, nutritional deficits, travel, and psychological stress, and whether commonly used biomarkers and experimental data adequately capture immune health in those performing arduous exercise. Both sides agreed that regular, moderate-duration exercise appears beneficial for immune defense, particularly in older and chronically ill individuals, while acknowledging that infection burden is notably high among elite athletes and military personnel. A point of consensus was that infection susceptibility is multifactorial in nature, but whether exercise per se causally drives increased infection risk in high-performance populations remains unresolved, with the authors calling for more rigorous empirical research to clarify this fundamental question in exercise immunology.

Spinal cord injury: Known and possible influences on the immune response to exercise

Christof A. Leicht, Victoria L. Goosey‐Tolfrey, Nicolette C. Bishop. Spinal cord injury: Known and possible influences on the immune response to exercise. PubMed. 2026

This review paper examined how spinal cord injury affects immune function, with a particular focus on the immune response to exercise. The authors conducted a comprehensive literature search across multiple databases to gather all relevant existing research on exercise immunology in individuals with spinal cord injury across patient, recreationally active, and athlete populations. Spinal cord injury was found to compromise immunity through multiple mechanisms, including reductions in lymphocyte numbers, disrupted autonomic function, and altered catecholamine concentrations, with individuals with tetraplegia being especially affected due to greater loss of sympathetic nervous system control over immune cells. However, some immune responses to exercise, such as increases in natural killer cell cytotoxicity and mucosal immunity changes in those with tetraplegia, appeared independent of catecholamine responses and similar to those seen in able-bodied individuals, suggesting that compensatory mechanisms such as sympathetic reflex activity, receptor hypersensitivity, or neuroendocrine adjustments may offset some injury-related immune deficits. The authors concluded that exercise and rehabilitation can enhance certain immune and autonomic measures following spinal cord injury, and called for further research to clarify the underlying mechanisms and the implications for infection susceptibility.

Understanding graft-versus-host disease. Preliminary findings regarding the effects of exercise in affected patients

Carmen Fiuza‐Luces, Nuria Garatachea, Nathan A. Berger et al.. Understanding graft-versus-host disease. Preliminary findings regarding the effects of exercise in affected patients. PubMed. 2026

This paper examined graft-versus-host disease, a serious multi-systemic complication arising from allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation in which donor immune cells attack host tissues, causing substantial morbidity and reduced physical capacity in long-term survivors. The authors reviewed the pathophysiological mechanisms underlying the disease, with particular attention to its chronic form, alongside available evidence from both animal and human studies on the effects of exercise as an adjunct therapy. Their review indicates that exercise training shows promise for improving physical fitness, potentially enhancing immune function, and reducing fatigue in affected patients. However, the authors conclude that the evidence base remains limited and that further randomized controlled trials focused specifically on this patient population are needed before firm recommendations can be made.

Disorders of Gut–Brain Interaction and the Rome V Process

Douglas A. Drossman, L Chang, Jan Tack. Disorders of Gut–Brain Interaction and the Rome V Process. Gastroenterology. 2026

This paper describes the development and scope of the Rome V criteria for disorders of gut–brain interaction, which are gastrointestinal conditions arising from disturbances in motility, visceral sensitivity, mucosal function, gut microbiota, or central nervous system processing. The Rome Foundation undertook a comprehensive revision building on the previous Rome IV framework, incorporating a decade of accumulated basic and clinical research. The update refined diagnostic definitions, introduced new evidence on the roles of diet, the gut microenvironment, pharmacogenomics, and biopsychosocial and sociocultural factors, and generated updated diagnostic algorithms. The process also addressed language issues by reducing imprecise or stigmatizing terminology and placed greater emphasis on the patient illness experience. The result is a broad, evidence-based framework intended to improve both the classification and therapeutic management of these conditions.

Osteoarthritis: molecular pathogenesis and potential therapeutic options

Yi Zhang, Yanqi Han, Ying Sun et al.. Osteoarthritis: molecular pathogenesis and potential therapeutic options. Signal Transduction and Targeted Therapy. 2026

This review examines the molecular pathogenesis of osteoarthritis, focusing on the cellular and organelle-level mechanisms that drive disease progression and associated pain. The authors synthesized current knowledge on nociceptive pathways connecting the knee joint to the central nervous system, inflammatory mediator activity, and dysfunction across three organelle systems: mitochondria, lysosomes, and the endoplasmic reticulum. They identify that mitochondrial dysfunction characterized by excessive reactive oxygen species and impaired energy production, lysosomal destabilization leading to chondrocyte apoptosis, and unresolved endoplasmic reticulum stress from compensatory protein overproduction collectively perpetuate cartilage degradation. Building on these mechanistic insights, the review outlines emerging pharmacological strategies that simultaneously target inflammatory cascades, restore organelle homeostasis through approaches such as mitophagy enhancement and lysosomal integrity preservation, and attenuate pain signaling. The authors also highlight organelle-targeted drug delivery systems as a means to improve therapeutic efficacy, proposing a multimodal framework aimed at addressing both the structural and clinical manifestations of osteoarthritis.

New approach methodologies for drug discovery

Wenqiang Liu, Paul Pang, Catherine A. Wu et al.. New approach methodologies for drug discovery. Cell. 2026

This review examines the growing field of new approach methodologies as alternatives to traditional animal-based drug discovery, which has been burdened by high failure rates. The authors survey recent advances in stem cell-based, organoid-based, and computational approaches that now cover the full drug-discovery continuum, from disease modeling and drug design through efficacy testing. The review highlights progress in identifying therapeutic candidates, developing novel models and technologies, and exploring NAMs themselves as potential treatments in preclinical and clinical settings. Key biological, technical, and regulatory bottlenecks limiting broader translational adoption are identified and discussed alongside evolving guidance from major regulatory and funding agencies. The authors conclude by outlining future human-centric discovery pipelines and the translational and societal considerations necessary for the responsible integration of these methodologies into mainstream drug development.

A Similarity Theorem and Its Breakdown in Atomic Black Hole Accretion

Marcus DuPont. A Similarity Theorem and Its Breakdown in Atomic Black Hole Accretion. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper investigates the mathematical structure of atomic gas accretion onto a point mass, demonstrating that the governing equations possess an exact similarity symmetry under a simultaneous rescaling of black hole mass and ambient density. The authors prove this rescaling is unique within its class and show it is preserved across time-dependent flows, two-body atomic microphysics, and a broad family of radiation and feedback prescriptions, leaving dimensionless flow profiles and Eddington ratios invariant. They introduce a dimensionless parameter measuring the fractional mass gained per Bondi time, which equivalently describes how fast the Bondi radius expands relative to the local sound speed, and show that sonic dilation marks the boundary where the similarity breaks down. For power-law accretion rates, the system reaches this breakdown after a finite mass increase and a bounded number of additional Bondi times, and the paper argues that canonical hyper-Eddington accretion scenarios have already crossed this threshold. The theorem is shown to unify radiating Bondi accretion and feedback-regulated scalings while identifying the regime where steady-state fixed-mass approximations lose validity.

Renormalization-guided inverse blocking for lattice field generation: construction and validation

Anna Hasenfratz, Ethan T. Neil, Letizia Parato et al.. Renormalization-guided inverse blocking for lattice field generation: construction and validation. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper proposes and validates a lattice field configuration generation algorithm that inverts a renormalization-group blocking transformation to efficiently produce configurations across multiple length scales. The method optimizes a blocking transformation under a "perfect blocking" condition so that the coarse-grained lattice can be described by a simple action, then uses a conditional normalizing flow to reconstruct the fine-scale degrees of freedom lost during decimation, followed by a brief rethermalization step to correct residual mismatches. The procedure is applied recursively in a cascade, reusing the same blocking transformation and learned flow to scale up from a small initial ensemble to much larger lattices without retraining. Testing in two-dimensional scalar field theory at criticality demonstrates stable upscaling across several orders of magnitude in lattice volume using only local computational resources. Rethermalization diagnostics confirm that short-distance mismatches relax quickly while long-distance (relevant) mismatches relax slowly, consistent with renormalization-group expectations, and the authors argue the framework extends naturally toward higher-dimensional, gauge, and fermionic theories.

QUEST: A Query and Extraction System for Topics in Asylum Law Application Decisions

Maria Vlachou, Anna Murphy Høgenhaug, Mohammad N. S. Jahromi et al.. QUEST: A Query and Extraction System for Topics in Asylum Law Application Decisions. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper addresses the challenge of automatically identifying credibility-related factors in asylum application appeal decisions, which are lengthy and complex legal documents. The authors developed QUEST, a system combining synthetic query generation, topic extraction, and relevance assessment to extract information pertaining to credibility indicators from two datasets of Danish asylum appeals. Beyond standard information retrieval evaluation metrics, they introduced a novel domain-specific evaluation framework that assesses system performance specifically with respect to credibility factors, rather than general relevance. Their findings show that evaluating system performance through credibility-based relevance assessments reveals greater difficulty than standard metrics suggest, highlighting the particular challenge of automating credibility analysis in asylum law contexts.

Offline-Verifiable Accountability for Cross-Organization Agent Messaging: A Preserved Evidence-Bundle Approach

Adil Alshammari, Hayretdin Bahsi. Offline-Verifiable Accountability for Cross-Organization Agent Messaging: A Preserved Evidence-Bundle Approach. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper addresses the challenge of verifying accountability in cross-organization agent-to-agent workflows when live systems are unavailable, controlled by a single party, or not trusted by all participants. The authors propose a preserved evidence-bundle model in which each workflow event carries all policy-required evidence—including sender authentication, delegation-aware authorization, log commitments, witness-backed checkpoints, and receiver-signed receipts—alongside a policy-controlled offline verifier that assesses evidence sufficiency without relying on transport behavior or platform-specific logs. The verifier is designed to accept only claims explicitly supported by the selected policy profile, providing a principled basis for later audit or dispute review. In a prototype evaluation spanning hundreds of complete workflows and thousands of preserved bundles, the system was tested across multiple policy profiles and evidence configurations, with checkpoint-context anchoring identified as the highest-latency component. Critically, all corrupted or policy-insufficient bundles were rejected with no false acceptances, supporting the approach's suitability for evidence-based accountability in adversarial or multi-party settings.

Analysis of Polynomial Threshold Functions on Random Regular Graphs: Computational Complexity of Detecting Noisy Random Lift

Xifan Yu. Analysis of Polynomial Threshold Functions on Random Regular Graphs: Computational Complexity of Detecting Noisy Random Lift. arXiv. 2026 preprint

The paper investigates the computational hardness of a hypothesis testing problem: distinguishing a noisy random lift of a base d-regular graph from a uniformly random d-regular graph. The authors analyze low-degree polynomial threshold functions applied to this detection problem, providing the first such analysis in this setting. As part of their approach, they derive new results on the distribution of short cycle counts in noisy random lifts up to logarithmic lengths, generalizing prior work on random regular graphs and random lifts. The findings contribute to understanding the statistical and computational complexity of detecting structured random graph models, situating the noisy random lift problem within the broader landscape of low-degree hardness results.

Understanding Venture Capital Syndication in Information Technology Sectors: A Network Formation Perspective

Liheng Tan, Zhengkai Tu, Prasanna Karhade. Understanding Venture Capital Syndication in Information Technology Sectors: A Network Formation Perspective. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper investigates how co-investment relationships form among venture capital firms operating in U.S. information technology sectors, examining the structural forces that shape syndication networks over time. Using comprehensive dyadic panel data spanning several decades across hardware, software, and hybrid subsectors, the authors tested seven theoretical mechanisms—including prior collaboration, shared partners, geographic proximity, and organizational similarity—through dyadic logit models and an exponential random graph model of a recent software investor network. The findings consistently identify prior collaboration as the strongest predictor of co-investment link formation, with shared partners, geographic proximity, and organizational-type similarity also positively associated with tie formation, while the influence of domain overlap, prominence, and experience varies by subsector. The whole-network model further confirms that triadic closure and geographic homophily structure the syndication landscape, illustrating how relational persistence and network closure jointly govern how venture capital partnerships coalesce in technology finance.

Biosensing technologies for foodborne pathogen detection and healthcare: principles, emerging materials, and intelligent platforms

Purshottam J. Assudani, Balakrishnan . P., Anny Leema A et al.. Biosensing technologies for foodborne pathogen detection and healthcare: principles, emerging materials, and intelligent platforms. Microchimica Acta. 2026

This review examines the current state and future directions of biosensing technologies designed to detect priority foodborne pathogens, including *Escherichia coli*, *Salmonella*, and *Listeria monocytogenes*, which continue to represent significant global public health threats. The authors surveyed advances across electrochemical, optical, and microfluidic sensing modalities, with particular attention to how functional nanomaterials, aptamers, nanozymes, and surface engineering strategies have improved sensor sensitivity, specificity, and performance in complex food matrices. A central theme is the integration of artificial intelligence and machine learning into biosensing platforms, enabling intelligent pattern recognition, real-time analytics, and high-throughput multiplexing that transforms conventional detection systems into smart diagnostic tools. The review critically identifies persistent challenges such as matrix interference, bioreceptor instability, manufacturing scalability, and regulatory standardization, while proposing mitigation strategies including ratiometric sensing, microfluidic pre-treatment, explainable AI, and printable electronics. Looking ahead, the authors advocate for self-powered biosensors, modular pathogen panel hubs, blockchain integration, and standardized validation pipelines as pathways toward regulation-ready platforms for next-generation food safety monitoring.

Advances in Pathogen Detection by Nanosensors: Biorecognition Strategies, Signal Amplification, and Platform Engineering

Aroofa Zargul, He Liu, Weiyu Zhang et al.. Advances in Pathogen Detection by Nanosensors: Biorecognition Strategies, Signal Amplification, and Platform Engineering. ACS Nano. 2026

This review paper examines the current state and emerging advances in nanosensor-based technologies for detecting pathogens in the context of growing infectious disease threats and antimicrobial resistance. The authors systematically survey three converging domains: biorecognition elements such as antibodies, aptamers, bacteriophages, antimicrobial peptides, molecularly imprinted polymers, and lectins that enable selective pathogen identification in complex biological samples; signal amplification strategies leveraging nanomaterials, enzymatic cascades, and isothermal nucleic acid amplification to achieve detection sensitivity down to single-cell and femtomolar levels; and integrated platform engineering combining CRISPR-Cas systems, artificial intelligence, and microfluidics for multiplexed point-of-care diagnostics. The review evaluates these advances against standardized performance metrics including detection limits, assay speed, specificity, and operational simplicity to identify both opportunities for synergy and persistent barriers to clinical translation. The authors conclude that these combined developments establish a strategic framework for next-generation diagnostic platforms capable of transforming infectious disease surveillance and enabling precision-guided treatment decisions.

Advances in tuberculosis diagnostics and biomarkers, 2020-26: scientific evidence, recommendations, gaps, and developmental priorities.

Walzl G, McHugh TD, Chakaya J et al.. Advances in tuberculosis diagnostics and biomarkers, 2020-26: scientific evidence, recommendations, gaps, and developmental priorities.. The Lancet. Microbe. 2027

This review synthesizes major advances in tuberculosis diagnostics and biomarker research from 2020 to 2025, examining how new evidence has shaped updated WHO policy guidance that consolidates recommendations for detecting tuberculosis disease and drug resistance within a unified framework. Digital chest radiography with computer-aided detection has become a core screening and triage tool, while decentralised molecular diagnosis has expanded through multiple classes of rapid nucleic acid amplification tests, including automated and manual platforms suited to low-resource settings. Novel specimen types such as tongue swabs, expanded use of non-sputum specimens in children, and programmatic innovations like sputum pooling have improved bacteriological confirmation in populations who cannot easily produce sputum, and targeted next-generation sequencing has been endorsed for comprehensive drug-susceptibility testing at reference laboratory level. Host blood transcriptomic signatures demonstrate promising prognostic and triage performance, and proteomic, metabolomic, and other biomarker classes are advancing through evaluation, yet no biomarker has met WHO target product profile criteria for a point-of-care test capable of reliably distinguishing infection from disease, including subclinical tuberculosis. The review concludes that significant gaps in diagnostic performance, implementation, and equity remain, defining priorities for future research and development.

Molecular Diagnostics of Infectious Diseases.

Takata M, Chikumi H, Yoshifuji A. Molecular Diagnostics of Infectious Diseases.. Yonago acta medica. 2026

This review paper examines the current landscape of molecular diagnostic technologies applied to the detection and characterization of infectious disease pathogens. The paper covers nucleic acid amplification approaches, including polymerase chain reaction variants optimized for speed, quantification, automation, and point-of-care use, as well as isothermal amplification methods that operate without thermal cycling equipment and are therefore suited to decentralized testing settings. CRISPR-based detection systems, which function at constant temperature and are often coupled with isothermal amplification, are presented as an emerging diagnostic strategy. Next-generation sequencing is described as a high-throughput approach capable of identifying novel pathogens, diagnosing complex infections, tracking outbreak transmission, monitoring pathogen evolution, and analyzing microbiomes as potential disease biomarkers. The authors conclude that future advances in molecular diagnostics will arise from integrating these complementary technologies into hybrid platforms that address the current limitations of each individual method.

SARS-CoV-2 Point-of-Care Testing Modalities: Integrating Molecular, Immunological, Biosensor, and AI Approaches.

Hetta HF, Ahmed R, Haseeb A et al.. SARS-CoV-2 Point-of-Care Testing Modalities: Integrating Molecular, Immunological, Biosensor, and AI Approaches.. Diagnostics (Basel, Switzerland). 2026

This review examined the landscape of point-of-care testing technologies developed for COVID-19 diagnosis, motivated by the pandemic's demonstration of the urgent need for rapid, decentralized diagnostic tools. The authors conducted a structured search of major scientific databases spanning the duration of the pandemic to evaluate molecular assays, antigen and antibody tests, biosensor platforms, and artificial intelligence-assisted diagnostic approaches. Molecular methods such as rapid RT-PCR, LAMP, and CRISPR-based technologies were found to offer high sensitivity and specificity suitable for decentralized use, while antigen tests provided fast and affordable screening with performance that varies by viral load, symptom timing, and variant. Antibody-based testing was deemed most useful for seroprevalence and immune monitoring rather than acute infection detection, and biosensor and AI-enabled systems showed promise but remained largely at prototype or early-validation stages requiring further clinical assessment. The review concludes that no single testing modality is universally optimal, and that combining complementary approaches alongside advances in multiplexing, digital connectivity, and variant-resilient detection will strengthen both COVID-19 management and future infectious disease preparedness.

AI-Enhanced Point-of-Care Diagnostics for Infectious Diseases in Resource-Limited Settings: A Scoping Review.

Farquhar H. AI-Enhanced Point-of-Care Diagnostics for Infectious Diseases in Resource-Limited Settings: A Scoping Review.. Tropical medicine & international health : TM & IH. 2026

This scoping review systematically mapped the landscape of artificial intelligence–enhanced point-of-care and rapid diagnostic technologies for infectious diseases in resource-limited settings, examining the extent of research, disease coverage, geographic representation, and validation rigor. Following established scoping review methodology and searching five major databases, the authors identified and analyzed over two hundred eligible studies published between early 2015 and early 2026. The field was found to be growing rapidly, though research was heavily concentrated on a narrow triad of diseases—COVID-19, malaria, and tuberculosis—while neglected tropical diseases were markedly underrepresented, and convolutional neural networks were the dominant artificial intelligence approach across diagnostic modalities. A critical finding was that the vast majority of studies lacked prospective field validation, with only a small fraction reporting this rigorous standard, and geographic coverage skewed toward East Africa and South Asia while West Africa and Latin America were underrepresented. The authors conclude that despite rapid growth, the field faces fundamental gaps in validation quality, disease equity, and geographic inclusivity, and they call for future work to prioritize real-world field validation, broaden disease focus, and meaningfully involve end-user communities from the earliest stages of research design.

Concordance Between Fingerprick and Venous Whole Blood CD4 Counts Using the Visitect CD4 Advanced disease platform.

Adzemovic T, Nalintya E, Onencan P et al.. Concordance Between Fingerprick and Venous Whole Blood CD4 Counts Using the Visitect CD4 Advanced disease platform.. The Journal of infectious diseases. 2026

This study evaluated whether fingerprick blood samples could serve as a reliable alternative to venous whole blood samples for CD4 testing using the Visitect CD4 Advanced Disease platform, a rapid semi-quantitative assay that classifies CD4 counts as above or below a clinically important threshold. The researchers conducted a prospective validation study directly comparing results obtained from the two blood collection methods on the same platform. They found a very high level of agreement between fingerprick and venous whole blood results, supporting the conclusion that fingerprick sampling with this assay could function as a dependable point-of-care diagnostic tool in resource-limited settings where conventional venous blood collection is not readily accessible.

An integrated centrifugal microfluidic CRISPR-based diagnostics platform for multiplexed point-of-care testing of respiratory pathogens.

Li L, Wei H, Wei M et al.. An integrated centrifugal microfluidic CRISPR-based diagnostics platform for multiplexed point-of-care testing of respiratory pathogens.. Biosensors & bioelectronics. 2026

The paper developed and validated a centrifugal microfluidic diagnostic platform called iCARD for multiplexed point-of-care detection of respiratory pathogens. The system integrates CRISPR/Cas13a chemistry with lyophilized reagents pre-loaded onto a spinning disc, paired with a portable fluorescence analyzer, and uses a novel resin-based nucleic acid purification method for throat swab samples. The platform can simultaneously screen six respiratory pathogens across four independent samples in a single automated run. Analytical testing confirmed single-copy-level sensitivity with no cross-reactivity, and clinical validation using retrospective patient samples demonstrated very high diagnostic sensitivity and perfect specificity. The authors conclude that iCARD offers a robust, accurate, and high-throughput solution for decentralized molecular diagnostics and epidemiological surveillance.

EULAR recommendations for the management of rheumatoid arthritis with synthetic and biologic disease-modifying antirheumatic drugs: 2025 update

Josef S. Smolen, Christopher J. Edwards, Victoria Konzett et al.. EULAR recommendations for the management of rheumatoid arthritis with synthetic and biologic disease-modifying antirheumatic drugs: 2025 update. Annals of the Rheumatic Diseases. 2026

This paper presents the updated clinical recommendations from a major European rheumatology alliance for managing rheumatoid arthritis using synthetic and biologic disease-modifying antirheumatic drugs. An international task force conducted systematic literature reviews on drug safety and efficacy, then used a structured voting process to revise and consolidate guidance from the previous iteration. The updated framework distilled recommendations into a streamlined set of principles covering conventional synthetic agents, glucocorticoids, biologic drugs, and targeted synthetic drugs including Janus kinase inhibitors. The recommendations follow a treat-to-target strategy beginning with methotrexate plus short-term glucocorticoids, escalating to biologic agents after insufficient response, with Janus kinase inhibitors considered as an alternative after careful evaluation of cardiovascular, malignancy, and thromboembolic risks. The guidance also addresses sequencing after treatment failure, tapering strategies in sustained remission, and cost considerations, with high levels of evidence and agreement achieved for most recommendations.

Hallie C. Prescott, Massimo Antonelli, Waleed Alhazzanic et al.. Surviving Sepsis Campaign: international guidelines for management of sepsis and septic shock 2026. Intensive Care Medicine. 2026

The abstract provided is too thin to summarize meaningfully, as it contains only background epidemiological context about sepsis and a brief statement about the importance of early identification and treatment, without describing the methods, specific recommendations, or findings of the 2026 guidelines themselves.

Smart wearable and implantable biosensors for continuous health monitoring: materials, biocompatibility, and AI integration

Thirumalaisamy Suryaprabha, Chunghyeon Choi, Yanfang Wu et al.. Smart wearable and implantable biosensors for continuous health monitoring: materials, biocompatibility, and AI integration. npj Flexible Electronics. 2026

This review examines smart wearable and implantable biosensors designed for continuous, real-time monitoring of biophysical and biochemical signals in the context of personalized and preventive healthcare. The paper surveys advances in flexible, stretchable, and biocompatible materials that facilitate long-term comfort and seamless integration with the body, alongside multimodal and multi-analyte sensing approaches that improve measurement robustness. It also addresses the role of artificial intelligence in enhancing signal processing and generating predictive health insights. The review identifies persistent challenges including motion artifacts, energy autonomy, data privacy, and clinical interpretability that must be overcome for broader adoption. Overall, the work synthesizes current progress in materials, device architectures, and AI-assisted strategies to outline the state of the field.

Data-Centric Foundation Models in Computational Healthcare: A Survey

Yunkun Zhang, Jin Gao, Zheling Tan et al.. Data-Centric Foundation Models in Computational Healthcare: A Survey. ACM Computing Surveys. 2026

This survey examines the intersection of foundation models and computational healthcare through a data-centric lens, focusing on how these AI systems can address longstanding challenges in clinical data acquisition, quality, and processing. The authors review a broad range of data-centric approaches spanning the full lifecycle of foundation models—from pre-training through inference—as applied to healthcare workflows. Key topics covered include data characterization, annotation, patient privacy, ethics, AI security, model assessment, and alignment with human values. The survey finds that the interactive, instruction-guided nature of foundation models offers a promising paradigm for improving clinical data pipelines and patient outcomes, while also highlighting persistent challenges unique to the healthcare domain. The authors conclude with an outlook on how foundation model-based analytics may transform clinical workflows as the field continues to evolve.

Exact quantification of nonlocal magic

Piotr Sierant. Exact quantification of nonlocal magic. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper addresses the question of how much "magic" (nonstabilizerness), the computational resource that enables universal quantum computation beyond Clifford circuits, is genuinely nonlocal—residing in correlations between two parties rather than in their individual local states. The key challenge had been that isolating nonlocal magic requires minimizing over all local bases, an optimization that previously had no exact solution. The authors solve this problem for the stabilizer fidelity, showing that the nonlocal magic of any pure multiqubit state equals the distance of its entanglement spectrum from the closest spectrum of Bell pairs. They also establish an unexpected connection to entanglement embezzlement: a family of states can universally embezzle entanglement under local operations and classical communication if and only if its nonlocal magic diverges, with the critical property being how the entanglement spectrum distributes weight across factor-of-two windows of rank rather than the total amount of entanglement. This distinction means that critical chains and random-singlet states, despite sharing the same logarithmic entanglement scaling, carry unbounded and vanishing nonlocal magic respectively, making nonlocal magic an operationally meaningful and experimentally accessible property of quantum correlations.

COVER: Identifiable Evaluation of Coalition Routing

Raghul Sugumar, Amrit Gopinath. COVER: Identifiable Evaluation of Coalition Routing. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper addresses a fundamental identification problem in evaluating multi-agent routing systems, where changing the team composition confounds outcome measurements and makes it difficult to isolate the effect of routing decisions from other system changes. The authors introduce an evaluation framework called COVER that fixes the information boundary, downstream processing stack, and allowable team configurations before outcomes are generated, enabling identifiable measurement of routing effects. The framework defines oracle regret as the gap between a chosen router's performance and the best available team, and establishes that executing the union of all distinct teams selected by competing policies is the minimal assumption-free basis for fair pairwise policy comparisons. Testing across multiple benchmarks and task environments, the framework successfully distinguished genuine routing improvements from spurious gains, revealing cases where raw accuracy improvements were negligible or statistically indistinguishable from zero while verified route quality improved substantially. The work positions COVER as an auditable measurement methodology that exposes selection headroom honestly rather than manufacturing apparent routing wins, and demonstrates that absolute performance scores depend on the downstream processing stack but that no detectable interaction exists between router choice and finalizer choice.

Acquire, Repair, Preserve: A Diagnosis-Guided Post-Training Recipe for Small-Model Dialogue Game Agents

Nan Li. Acquire, Repair, Preserve: A Diagnosis-Guided Post-Training Recipe for Small-Model Dialogue Game Agents. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper investigates how to improve small language models as agents in interactive dialogue games, where a model must track state across turns, interpret feedback, and select valid actions under evolving constraints. The authors diagnosed failures of a small open-weight model in the LM Playschool Challenge, finding that errors fell into two categories: broad gaps in game participation knowledge and local decision failures such as repeated guesses, malformed actions, and ignoring recently received feedback. Based on these diagnostics, they designed a three-step post-training recipe: supervised fine-tuning to acquire general game participation ability, targeted preference learning on turn-local pairs to repair mechanically detectable failures within a specific dialogue-game family, and preservation of general capabilities outside the game domain. Applying this recipe substantially improved both public and closed in-domain scores while approximately maintaining aggregate static benchmark performance. The authors conclude that broad supervised fine-tuning accounts for most capability gains, that turn-local preference supervision is effective when failure modes can be precisely detected, and that transfer of these improvements remains largely within the targeted game family rather than generalizing broadly.

Prompt-Guided Interactive Segmentation of Interstitial Lung Disease in Thoracic CT

Vasilis Dedousis, Lubnaa Abdur Rahman, Lorenzo Brigatο et al.. Prompt-Guided Interactive Segmentation of Interstitial Lung Disease in Thoracic CT. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper investigated prompt-guided interactive segmentation of interstitial lung disease patterns in thoracic CT imaging, addressing the limitations of existing methods that rely on dense annotations and produce static, non-refinable predictions. The authors adapted MedSAM2, a promptable foundation model, for three-dimensional ILD segmentation and evaluated three fine-tuning strategies alongside multiple clinically motivated prompt types, including bounding boxes, points, lasso, and scribble interactions, across seven ILD patterns and healthy lung tissue. Full model fine-tuning yielded the best performance, improving average Dice scores over the baseline MedSAM2 model, with bounding box prompts achieving the strongest results while non-native prompt types such as lasso and scribble also proved effective. The authors additionally demonstrated a proof-of-concept end-to-end workflow in which the model is initialized from an automatic segmentation prior and subsequently refined through radiologist prompts, offering a practical path toward clinical integration of interactive ILD assessment.

When Linguistic and Internal Confidence Diverge in Large Language Models

Hefan Zhang, Bingquan Zhang, Ming Cheng et al.. When Linguistic and Internal Confidence Diverge in Large Language Models. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper investigated whether the confidence expressed linguistically by large language models matches their internal confidence as measured by model-intrinsic signals. The researchers compared linguistic confidence against logits-based confidence for classification tasks and semantic-entropy-based uncertainty for generation tasks across a large set of models from multiple families, examining association, magnitude agreement, and calibration as distinct evaluative axes. They found that linguistic and internal confidence frequently diverge, with weak instance-level association on average, though alignment improves for easier items and stronger base models. Instruction-tuned models tend to report inflated confidence and show larger gaps and worse calibration, while prompt manipulations such as attitude cues worsen alignment even when they shift confidence distributions. The authors conclude that linguistic confidence operates as a lossy channel—a dispersed verbal distribution can carry rank-order information without achieving calibration—and advocate for multi-axis diagnostic evaluation before linguistic confidence is used in downstream reliability applications.

Localizing Global Discrepancies: Marginal Contributions and Contextual Anomaly Detection

Tommaso dorigo. Localizing Global Discrepancies: Marginal Contributions and Contextual Anomaly Detection. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper addresses the problem of identifying which individual observations drive a detected departure from a reference distribution, given that global goodness-of-fit statistics can confirm a discrepancy exists but cannot localize it. The authors develop a theoretical framework by assigning each observation a contribution score based on its conditional or marginal role across random statistical contexts, connecting ideas from resampling diagnostics, data valuation, projection theory, and anomaly detection. They establish formal relationships showing that for symmetric statistics this localization is equivalent to centered conditional localization, that for U-statistics the contribution equals the first Hoeffding–Hájek term, and that for maximum mean discrepancy the approach reduces to the MMD witness function. Empirical evaluation on a particle physics anomaly-detection benchmark demonstrates that their pair-based estimator converges to the direct empirical MMD witness with theoretically predicted scaling, achieving very high correlation and nearly identical classification performance. The paper further distinguishes two distinct roles that context can play: efficiently localizing a known global discrepancy versus providing genuinely additional discriminating information when the alternative hypothesis contains shared latent structure that makes individual-event detection impossible but ensemble-level detection feasible.
